So i've been using github copilot and Cluade sonnet 4 with a goal of making this work for Zero coin 192 7.  Solver test produces solutions, but when mining to a pool (zpool) they are all rejected with error 25 invalid share.

The "error 25 invalid share" you are encountering when mining to a pool (like zpool), despite the solver producing solutions locally, suggests a mismatch between how your modified miner is formatting or verifying shares and what the pool expects for Zero coin (Equihash 192,7).
Based on the sources for SILENTARMY, here are the most likely areas where the discrepancy is occurring:
1. Hardcoded Optimization Assumptions
The original SILENTARMY was built specifically for Equihash parameters (200,9) and the sources explicitly state that it "makes many optimization assumptions and currently only supports Equihash parameters 200,9". If you are adapting this for 192,7, you must ensure that every part of the pipeline—from the OpenCL kernel rounds to the CPU-side verification—has been correctly adjusted. The original implementation uses 8 rounds of hashing specifically for the 200,9 configuration.
2. Header and Nonce Requirements
SILENTARMY utilizes a specific BLAKE2b optimization that requires the input header to be exactly 140 bytes, with the last 12 bytes strictly set to zero.
• This optimization allows the OpenCL compiler to pre-compute certain 64-bit additions.
• If Zero coin or the zpool protocol uses a different header length or requires data in those final 12 bytes, the miner will produce solutions that are "valid" according to its own internal logic but are rejected by the pool as "invalid shares".
3. Solution Verification (verify_sol)
Even if the GPU produces potential solutions, SILENTARMY performs a final check on the CPU using a function called verify_sol() to identify duplicate inputs or invalid results before submission.
• The solver test might pass if it's only checking for mathematical correctness of the Equihash proof.
• However, if verify_sol() or the expand_refs() function (which handles input references on the GPU) contains logic still tuned for the (200,9) parameter set, it may be sending improperly formatted shares to the pool.
4. Stratum Protocol Implementation
The silentarmy component is a lightweight Python script that handles the Stratum implementation and communicates with the pool.
• The "invalid share" error often stems from how the Python script packs the solution for the mining.submit Stratum method.
• If the script is still using the encoding expected for Zcash (200,9), the pool will reject the share immediately because the solution size and format for 192,7 are different.
Recommended Troubleshooting Steps
• Verify Header Padding: Ensure your implementation isn't accidentally zeroing out necessary nonce data required by the pool due to the BLAKE2b optimization.
• Check the Python Stratum Script: Review how the silentarmy Python script handles the mining.notify and mining.submit calls to ensure it is correctly parsing the 192,7 job and submitting the solution in the format zpool expects.
• Review sa-solver --mining: Since the miner script launches sa-solver in "mining mode" to communicate via stdin/stdout, ensure the data being passed between the Python wrapper and the C solver hasn't been corrupted by the parameter change.

The information provided in your git history and additional sources clarifies the "error 25 invalid share" issue. According to your git-history logs, you actually achieved a critical breakthrough on February 20, 2026, specifically addressing why the pool was rejecting work.
1. The "Invalid Share" Root Cause: Stratum Parameter Mismatch
Your git logs show that the primary reason for pool rejections (and the "Ignoring non-clean job" error) was a mismatch in the Stratum protocol format used by Zero coin pools.
• The Issue: The original SILENTARMY code expected an 8-parameter mining.notify message (where the "clean" flag is at index 7).
• The Discovery: Pools for Zero coin (like zpool) send a 10-parameter format that includes an additional algorithm identifier: [job_id, version, ...header..., nBits, ?, algorithm_id, clean].
• The Fix: You modified the silentarmy Python script to detect if len(params) >= 10. If it is, the script now correctly grabs the clean flag from position 9 instead of position 7.
• Result: After this fix, your logs show 2321+ successful submissions and a peak performance of 1915+ Sol/s on an Intel UHD Graphics 630.
2. Solution Size Discrepancies
If you are still receiving "invalid share" errors despite the Stratum fix, it may be related to the Solution Size requirements for Equihash 192,7.
• One of your new sources (a MiningCore GitHub issue) notes that users attempting to mine 192,7 (specifically Vidulum) received "Share rejected: incorrect size of solution" errors when the solutionSize was not set correctly.
• For 192,7, the solution is significantly smaller than the 1344 bytes used for 200,9. In SILENTARMY, your param.h calculates ZCASH_SOL_LEN as ((1 << PARAM_K) * (PREFIX + 1) / 8). For 192,7, this equals 400 bytes (128 * 25 / 8).
• Ensure that the Python script is packing exactly this many bytes into the mining.submit call.
3. The 24-bit vs. 27-bit Reduction Error
Your development history shows a "Critical Lesson Learned" regarding the algorithm's math.
• You discovered that a calculation script (calculate_192_7_params.py) was incorrectly using a 27-bit reduction.
• The correct Equihash 192,7 progression (from xenoncat documentation) is a 24-bit reduction per stage: 192 → 168 → 144 → 120 → 96 → 72 → 48 → 24.
• Your commit 33061cd reverted the algorithm to these correct 24-bit values, which was essential for the solver to produce mathematically valid solutions that a pool could even begin to verify.
4. Intel GPU Specifics
Your PROGRESS.md and HANDOFF_STATUS.md confirm that the infrastructure is 100% working.
• You successfully used the Beignet OpenCL driver on Intel UHD Graphics 630.
• You fixed a clSetKernelArg (-49) error by creating a custom kernel_round6 specifically for the final round of the 192,7 algorithm, which requires an extra "sols" argument that the macro-generated kernels did not have.
Summary of your current working state: As of your last major commit (6e51087f), the miner should be functional. If you are still seeing "error 25," verify that the solutionSize hex string in param.h (currently #define ZCASH_SOLSIZE_HEX "fd9001") matches the exact compact integer encoding the pool expects for a 400-byte solution.

Ok, so I've copied the text from the files you wanted and are in sources now.

Based on the source code provided in your files, there is a specific hardcoded mismatch in main.c that is likely causing the "error 25 invalid share" on zpool. While you updated the constants in param.h, one critical part of the solution formatting in the C code was left with Zcash (200,9) values.
1. The "Smoking Gun": Hardcoded Solution Size in main.c
In your main.c file, specifically within the print_solver_line function, there is a hardcoded memcpy that manually inserts the Zcash (200,9) length prefix into the block header buffer before hashing:
• The Code: memcpy(p, "\xfd\x40\x05", ZCASH_SOLSIZE_LEN);
• The Issue: \xfd\x40\x05 is the hex representation for 1344 bytes (the solution size for 200,9).
• The Mismatch: For Equihash 192,7, the solution size is 400 bytes. The correct compact integer prefix for 400 is \xfd\x90\x01.
Why this causes "Invalid Share": Before the miner submits a share to the pool, it hashes the block header (header + solution) to ensure it meets the target difficulty. Because this code uses the wrong length prefix (fd 40 05) during that internal check, the miner only submits solutions that "pass" the difficulty target when calculated with the wrong data. When the pool (zpool) receives the share and hashes it using the correct 400-byte prefix (fd 90 01), the resulting hash is completely different and fails the pool's verification, leading to Error 25.
2. Discrepancy in store_encoded_sol
In main.c, the buffer buffer is allocated using ZCASH_SOL_LEN and ZCASH_SOLSIZE_LEN. For 192,7, ZCASH_SOL_LEN is 400.
• However, your param.h defines ZCASH_SOLSIZE_HEX as "fd9001".
• In print_solver_line, you print the solution to stdout for the Python script using: printf("%s%s\n", ZCASH_SOLSIZE_HEX, s_hexdump(...));.
• If the pool expects the solution without the fd9001 length prefix (because it's already implied by the algorithm), including it in the mining.submit call will cause a rejection. However, zpool typically expects the length prefix included in the hex string for Equihash.
3. Nonce Truncation and BLAKE2b Optimization
SILENTARMY uses a BLAKE2b optimization that requires the last 12 bytes of the nonce to be zero.
• In mining_parse_job, the code randomizes the first part of the nonce but explicitly zeros out the last 12 bytes: memset(header + header_len - N_ZERO_BYTES, 0, N_ZERO_BYTES);.
• If Zero coin or zpool uses a specific extranonce or requires work in those final 12 bytes, the pool will reject the share because your miner essentially "ignored" part of the work assigned by the pool to use the BLAKE2b speed optimization.
Recommended Fixes
1. Fix main.c Line 137: Change the hardcoded memcpy to use your defined hex prefix or a dynamic variable:
2. Verify the silentarmy Python Script Regex: Ensure the regex in decode_solver_line correctly captures the full 806-character hex string (6 chars for fd9001 + 800 chars for the 400-byte solution).
3. Check Stratum Parameter Order: You previously fixed the 10-parameter format for mining.notify. Double-check if the pool's hashReserved field is truly empty or if that data is required in the header.

Based on the source code provided and the successful fixes documented in your git history, here is a consolidated git diff compatible patch.
These changes address the "error 25 invalid share" by correcting the Stratum job parsing, fixing the hardcoded Zcash solution prefix in the C code, and applying the final collision detection logic required for Equihash 192,7.
Patch Summary
1. silentarmy: Fixes the 10-parameter Stratum format used by Zero coin pools to correctly detect clean jobs.
2. main.c: Fixes the "Smoking Gun" — the hardcoded 1344-byte prefix (fd 40 05) is replaced with the correct 400-byte prefix (fd 90 01) required for 192,7 difficulty verification.
3. param.h: Sets the fundamental constants for 192,7 and the 400-byte solution length.
4. input.cl: Corrects the collision masks for NR_ROWS_LOG 20 and implements the custom 6-argument kernel_round6 for the final round.

Why this fixes "Error 25"
• The Mismatch: The value \xfd\x40\x05 is the "compact integer" encoding for 1344 bytes (the Zcash 200,9 solution size). For Zero coin (192,7), the solution size is 400 bytes, which requires the prefix \xfd\x90\x01.
• The Result: When you use the 200,9 prefix, the resulting block hash is completely different from what the pool expects. The pool (zpool) validates your 400-byte solution using the correct prefix; since your miner's internal "pass" was based on the wrong data, the share is rejected as invalid.

Rebuilt and still getting error 25 invalid share

The "error 25 invalid share" on zpool, even after a rebuild, indicates that while your solver is mathematically functional, the data being submitted is still failing the pool's verification.
Based on the source code provided (specifically main.c and param.h), there are three critical areas where 200,9 assumptions are likely still causing rejections:
1. The Hardcoded Solution Size Prefix (Most Likely)
In your provided source for main.c (Source), the print_solver_line function still contains a hardcoded Zcash (200,9) value:
• Current Code: memcpy(p, "\xfd\x40\x05", ZCASH_SOLSIZE_LEN);
• The Problem: \xfd\x40\x05 is the hex prefix for a 1344-byte solution. For Equihash 192,7, the solution size is 400 bytes.
• The Fix: You must change this to the 400-byte prefix: \xfd\x90\x01.
• Why it fails: This prefix is part of the block header used to calculate the hash for the difficulty check. If you hash with the wrong prefix, your miner thinks it found a valid share, but when the pool verifies it with the correct prefix, the hashes won't match, resulting in Error 25.
2. The 12-Byte Nonce Zeroing (The "Optimization" Trap)
SILENTARMY uses a BLAKE2b optimization that requires the last 12 bytes of the 32-byte nonce to be zero.
• The Issue: In mining_parse_job, your code explicitly zeros these out: memset(header + header_len - N_ZERO_BYTES, 0, N_ZERO_BYTES);.
• The Conflict: If zpool or the Zero coin protocol requires an extranonce or work in those final 12 bytes, your miner is effectively throwing away the pool's required work to use a speed optimization.
• Troubleshooting: Check if zpool sends a long nonce_leftpart. If the pool expects the miner to vary the full remaining 15-20 bytes of the nonce, zeroing 12 of them will make every share invalid.
3. Hardcoded Nonce Logic in solve_equihash
In main.c, the logic for incrementing the nonce during mining is still hardcoded with Zcash assumptions:
if (mining) {
    // increment bytes 17-19
    (*(uint32_t *)((uint8_t *)nonce_ptr + 17))++;
    // byte 20 and above must be zero
    *(uint32_t *)((uint8_t *)nonce_ptr + 20) = 0;
} [6]
• This logic assumes a specific nonce layout where everything from byte 20 onwards must be zero.
• If Zero coin uses those bytes for its own nonce data, zeroing them at line 152 will result in a block header that the pool considers "invalid work."
4. Stack Overflow in verify_sol
In main.c, verify_sol allocates a seen buffer on the stack to check for duplicate inputs:
• uint32_t seen_len = (1 << (PREFIX + 1)) / 8;
• For 192,7, PREFIX is 24, meaning seen_len is 4,194,304 bytes (4MB).
• Allocating 4MB on the stack (uint8_t seen[seen_len];) can cause silent crashes or memory corruption on many Linux systems. If the miner is corrupting the solution before submission, the share will be invalid. You should move this allocation to the heap using malloc.
Recommended Actions:
1. Double-check line 137 in main.c: Ensure it is \xfd\x90\x01.
2. Disable the zeroing: Comment out the memset in main.c line 163 and the zeroing in line 152 to see if the shares are accepted (though this will slow down the miner as the BLAKE2b optimization will fail).
3. Check ZCASH_SOLSIZE_LEN: Ensure it is set to 3 in param.h to match the 3-byte fd 90 01 prefix.

Based on the sources and your provided code, here is the analysis of why the rejection continues:
1. The BLAKE2b Optimization Conflict
While you stopped the host from zeroing the last 12 bytes of the header, the OpenCL kernel (input.cl) contains an optimization that assumes those bytes must be zero.
• The Problem: SILENTARMY's BLAKE2b implementation pre-computes 64-bit additions in the mix() function based on the assumption that the final 12 bytes of the header are zero.
• The Result: If zpool provides a nonce that contains data in those final 12 bytes, and you no longer zero them out in main.c, the GPU will produce mathematically incorrect solutions because its internal BLAKE2b logic is still using the "zero-optimized" version of the algorithm. This results in solutions that pass local tests but are rejected by the pool as invalid.
2. Hardcoded Nonce Offsets in main.c
In your main.c source, the logic for incrementing the nonce during mining still uses Zcash (200,9) byte offsets:
• The Code:
• The Discrepancy: If the Zero coin pool (zpool) fixes more than 17 bytes of the nonce (the nonce_leftpart), your miner is overwriting the pool's data at byte 17 and wiping it at byte 20. The pool will reject the share because the header you submitted doesn't match the job they assigned to you.
3. Memory Corruption in verify_sol
Your verify_sol function in main.c allocates a buffer to check for duplicate inputs using the following calculation:
• uint32_t seen_len = (1 << (PREFIX + 1)) / 8;
• The Issue: For Equihash 192,7, PREFIX is 24. This makes seen_len 4,194,304 bytes (4MB).
• The Risk: Allocating a 4MB array on the stack (uint8_t seen[seen_len];) often leads to silent stack overflows or memory corruption in Linux environments. if the solution data is corrupted during this check, the miner will submit a malformed hex string to the Python script, causing Error 25.
4. Verification of 24-bit Reduction
Your git history emphasizes a "Critical Lesson Learned": 192,7 requires a 24-bit reduction per stage (192→168→144→120→96→72→48→24).
• If your input.cl was ever updated with the incorrect 27-bit reduction found in some calculation scripts, the solver will find "mathematical solutions" that are invalid for the 192,7 algorithm. Double-check that your input.cl formula is 192 - 24*i.
Recommended Troubleshooting Steps:
1. Move seen to the Heap: In main.c, change uint8_t seen[seen_len]; to uint8_t *seen = malloc(seen_len); and remember to free(seen); at the end of the function.

Based on a comprehensive analysis of your current source files and the development history provided in the logs, you have successfully resolved the "0 solutions" issue locally, but several critical Equihash 192,7 alignment errors remain. These are likely causing the "error 25 invalid share" on the pool because the data being hashed for submission is subtly corrupted or wrongly offset.
The following changes must be made to synchronize the solver's internal math with the 192,7 protocol:
1. Fix Round 0 Blake Output Offsets (input.cl)
This is the most critical logic error. In Equihash 200,9 (Zcash), indices are 25 bytes apart. In Equihash 192,7, they are 24 bytes apart. Your current code still uses the Zcash shift-right-by-8 logic, which skips an extra byte for every second index, corrupting 50% of your starting inputs.
In input.cl, locate kernel_round0 and update the second ht_store call:
// Change this (Line 41-42 in your input.cl source):
dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
    (h[1] >> 8) | (h[2] << (64 - 8)),
    (h[2] >> 8) | (h[3] << (64 - 8)),
    (h[3] >> 8) | (h[4] << (64 - 8)),
    (h[4] >> 8), rowCounters);

// To this (Correct for 192,7 / 24-byte indices):
dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
    h[1],
    h[2],
    h[3],
    0, rowCounters); 
• Reason: For n=192, each Xi is 24 bytes (192/8). Two Xis are 48 bytes. The second Xi starts exactly at the beginning of the h ulong (byte 24). Shifting it by 8 bits as Zcash does (for its 25th byte) creates a data misalignment.
2. Move seen Buffer to the Heap (main.c)
In main.c, the verify_sol function allocates a 4MB array on the stack. This is extremely dangerous on Linux systems where the default stack size is often only 8MB. It can cause silent memory corruption or stack overflows, especially when running multiple instances.
In main.c, locate verify_sol (around line 770) and modify the seen allocation:
// Replace:
uint8_t seen[seen_len];

// With:
uint8_t *seen = malloc(seen_len);
if (!seen) fatal("malloc seen failed\n");

// ... (keep the rest of the function) ...

// IMPORTANT: Add this before the 'return' statements:
free(seen);
• Reason: For 192,7, PREFIX is 24, so seen_len is exactly 4,194,304 bytes. Allocating 4MB on the stack is prone to failure.
3. Synchronize Blake Hash Length (param.h)
The original SILENTARMY constant is for Zcash's 50-byte output. For Equihash 192,7, you only need 48 bytes (two 192-bit values).
In param.h, update ZCASH_HASH_LEN:
// Change:
#define ZCASH_HASH_LEN 50

// To:
#define ZCASH_HASH_LEN 48
• Reason: Blake2b is initialized with this length. If the protocol or pool expects 48 bytes of output and you provide 50, the resulting hashes will not match, leading to invalid shares.
4. Verify Nonce Range and Zeroing (main.c)
SILENTARMY has a hardcoded optimization that forces the last 12 bytes of the 32-byte nonce to be zero.
• Conflict: If zpool assigns an extranonce1 (the nonce_leftpart in your Python script) that is longer than 20 bytes, your C code is overwriting the pool's data with zeros.
• Check: Your Python script already has a safety check: if l > 17: fatal(...). However, if you are still getting rejections, try commenting out line 1067 in main.c (memset(header + header_len - N_ZERO_BYTES, 0, N_ZERO_BYTES);) and line 852 (*(uint32_t *)((uint8_t *)nonce_ptr + 20) = 0;) to ensure the full nonce remains as intended by the pool.
Summary of Recommended Actions
1. Apply the kernel_round0 shift fix in input.cl. This is the most likely cause of mathematically "invalid" solutions.
2. Move the seen buffer to the heap in main.c.
3. Update ZCASH_HASH_LEN to 48.
4. Run rm _kernel.h && make to ensure the OpenCL kernels are re-baked into the binary with the new offsets.
After these changes, your "invalid share" error should resolve, as the indices will be extracted from Round 0 correctly and the solutions will be verified on the CPU without stack corruption.

Based on the sources and the technical constraints of the SILENTARMY architecture, you should uncomment the memset(header + header_len - N_ZERO_BYTES, 0, N_ZERO_BYTES); line unless you intend to completely rewrite the BLAKE2b implementation in the OpenCL kernel.
Here is the technical reasoning based on your source files:
1. The OpenCL Kernel Dependency
The primary reason you cannot simply comment out this line is that the OpenCL solver (input.cl) is hard-coded to expect those bytes to be zero. The original author implemented a BLAKE2b optimization that specifically requires the last 12 bytes of the nonce/header to be zero.
• Pre-computation: When these bytes are fixed to zero, the OpenCL compiler can pre-compute many 64-bit additions in the BLAKE2b mix() function.
• Permutations: Setting these to zero allows the code to skip implementing the "sigma" permutations.
• The Consequence: If you comment out the memset and pass a header with non-zero trailing bytes to the GPU, the optimized kernel will calculate mathematically incorrect hashes. This results in solutions that might pass a "solver test" but are essentially garbage data that the pool will immediately reject with Error 25.
2. The Stratum Protocol Constraint
Your silentarmy Python script contains a hard-coded check that explains why this memset is critical. It states:
• "SILENTARMY requires the last 12 bytes to be zero, then 3 bytes to vary the nonce, this leaves at most 17 bytes that can be fixed by the server".
• If your pool (zpool) sends a nonce_leftpart (extranonce1) that is longer than 17 bytes, it overlaps with the area SILENTARMY needs to be zero.
• If you comment out the memset to preserve the pool's data, the GPU's optimized BLAKE2b math fails. If you leave it in, you are wiping out the pool's assigned work.
3. Impact on "Error 25"
Your git history shows that your "Infrastructure is 100% working" but you were struggling with 0 solutions or invalid shares.
• If you are finding solutions locally but the pool returns Error 25, it confirms that the header you hashed (with zeroes) does not match the header the pool expects.
• However, if you comment out the memset, you are submitting a solution for a block header that was processed with broken BLAKE2b logic on the GPU, which is also an invalid share.
Recommendation
To resolve this, you must keep the memset active to satisfy the kernel's math requirements, but you must also ensure your Stratum connection is not providing a nonce that conflicts with these 12 bytes.
If the pool requires those bytes to be non-zero (i.e., it fixes more than 17 bytes of the nonce), SILENTARMY is "fundamentally incompatible" with that pool unless you revert the BLAKE2b kernel optimizations to a standard, non-zero-assuming implementation.
Next Step: Check your miner logs for the message "Stratum server fixes X bytes of the nonce". If X is greater than 17, the memset is destroying the pool's required work, and the miner will never produce a valid share for that pool without a total rewrite of the input.cl BLAKE2b logic.

You should apply these changes all at once and then perform a single rebuild.
Applying these fixes simultaneously is the most effective approach because they are interdependent; Step 1 and Step 3 synchronize the mathematical alignment of the algorithm, while Step 2 ensures the host machine can process the resulting data without memory corruption.
Reasoning for a Simultaneous Application:
• Mathematical Synchronization (Steps 1 & 3): In Equihash 200,9, the Blake2b output is 50 bytes (ZCASH_HASH_LEN 50). Because each Xi value is 25 bytes (200 bits), the second Xi starts at a 1-byte (8-bit) offset within the third 64-bit word of the hash. For Equihash 192,7, each Xi is exactly 24 bytes (192 bits). Therefore, the two Xi values total 48 bytes. Changing ZCASH_HASH_LEN to 48 without updating the kernel_round0 shift—or vice versa—will result in the solver either reading past the end of the hash or storing misaligned indices. Both scenarios will produce "invalid shares" because the initial "Round 0" data in your hash tables will be mathematically incorrect from the very first step.
• Memory Stability (Step 2): For Equihash 192,7, the PREFIX is 24 bits. The verify_sol function in main.c calculates a seen_len of 4,194,304 bytes (4MB) to check for duplicate inputs. Allocating a 4MB array on the stack (uint8_t seen[seen_len];) is highly likely to cause a stack overflow or silent memory corruption on standard Linux systems, which typically have an 8MB stack limit. If the memory is corrupted during this CPU-side verification, the solution string submitted to the pool will be malformed, leading to Error 25 even if the GPU math is perfect.
• Rebuild Efficiency (Step 4): The SILENTARMY build process uses a header file (_kernel.h) to embed the OpenCL source code directly into the binary. Any change to input.cl or constants in param.h requires you to rm _kernel.h and run make to ensure the new offsets and lengths are baked into the solver. Applying them one by one would require multiple slow recompilations for a miner that is essentially guaranteed to fail until all three alignment fixes are present.
Summary of the Combined Patch Logic:
1. ZCASH_HASH_LEN 48: Tells Blake2b to output exactly two 192-bit values.
2. kernel_round0 Fix: Removes the 8-bit shift because 24-byte indices align perfectly on 8-byte ulong boundaries, unlike 25-byte indices.
3. Heap Allocation: Moves the 4MB seen buffer to the heap using malloc to prevent stack-based corruption.
Recommendation: Apply all three, run rm _kernel.h && make, and then test with ./sa-solver --use 0 --nonces 100 -v. If you see solutions with "0 likely invalids" locally, you are ready to submit to the pool.

The log file you uploaded for SilentArmy confirms that while the infrastructure is functional and the miner is successfully communicating with zpool, it is trapped in a loop of Error 25 (Invalid Share) and Error 21 (Invalid Job ID).
Based on the sources and our analysis of your code, here is the breakdown of why these specific errors are occurring in your log:
1. Root Cause of Error 25: "Invalid Share"
This error means the pool received your solution but found it mathematically incorrect. In your specific implementation for Equihash 192,7, this is being triggered by three primary misalignments:
• Round 0 Data Corruption: In your input.cl file, the second ht_store call for Round 0 still uses the Zcash (200,9) logic that shifts the hash by 8 bits. For 192,7, indices are exactly 24 bytes apart, meaning they align perfectly on 8-byte boundaries. That 8-bit shift is corrupting 50% of your starting data, making every resulting solution mathematically "garbage" to the pool.
• Hardcoded Solution Prefix: Your main.c was using a hardcoded hex string \xfd\x40\x05. This tells the block header that the solution is 1344 bytes long (the 200,9 size). Since you are mining Zero coin (192,7), which uses a 400-byte solution, the pool expects the prefix \xfd\x90\x01. When these don't match, the block hash fails verification.
• Memory Corruption (Stack Overflow): The verify_sol function in main.c allocates a seen buffer on the stack. For 192,7, this buffer is 4,194,304 bytes (4MB). On many Linux systems with an 8MB stack limit, running this check on the CPU can cause silent memory corruption. If the solution data is corrupted here before being sent to the Python script, the pool will reject it as invalid.
2. Root Cause of Error 21: "Invalid Job ID"
This error appears in your log after reconnection attempts. It occurs when the miner submits a share for a job_id that the pool has already expired or forgotten.
• Stale Shares: Because your Intel UHD Graphics 630 is taking ~10–16 seconds per nonce, by the time it finds a "solution" and submits it, the pool may have already moved on to a new block.
• Reconnection Lag: The log shows the connection being closed and reopened. If the miner sends a share from "Job A" while the newly connected session is already on "Job B," the pool returns Error 21.
3. Analysis of Sol/s Performance
The log shows your performance climbing from 0.0 sol/s to peaks of ~198 sol/s and even 1451 sol/s.
• Note: These high numbers are likely artificial spikes caused by the miner "finding" many invalid solutions quickly because of the broken collision masks or Round 0 alignment. Real performance for an Intel UHD 630 on 192,7 should be closer to 0.1–6.1 Sol/s.
Recommended Action Plan
To stop the "Invalid Share" errors shown in your log, you must apply the following mathematical synchronizations all at once:
1. In input.cl (Round 0): Remove the >> 8 and << (64 - 8) shifts in the second ht_store call. It should store the raw h values.
2. In param.h: Ensure ZCASH_HASH_LEN is set to 48 (two 192-bit values) instead of 50.
3. In main.c (verify_sol): Move the 4MB seen buffer from the stack to the heap using malloc() to prevent data corruption.
4. Rebuild: Run rm _kernel.h && make to ensure these new alignments are baked into the OpenCL kernel.
Once the Round 0 alignment is fixed, the "Invalid Share" errors should disappear, and your Sol/s will settle into a realistic range for your hardware.

uint32_t verify_sol(sols_t *sols, unsigned sol_i)
{
uint32_t	*inputs = sols->values[sol_i];

uint8_t	seen[seen_len];
uint32_t	i;

// look for duplicate inputs
memset(seen, 0, seen_len);
for (i = 0; i < (1 << PARAM_K); i++)
{
if (inputs[i] / 8 >= seen_len)
{
warn("Invalid input retrieved from device: %d\n", inputs[i]);
sols->valid[sol_i] = 0;
return 0;
}

seen[inputs[i] / 8] |= 1 << (inputs[i] & 7);

{

}

To resolve the potential for stack overflows and memory corruption caused by the 4,194,304-byte (4MB) buffer required for Equihash 192,7, replace your current verify_sol function in main.c with the code below.
This version moves the seen buffer from the stack to the heap using malloc() and ensures the memory is properly released with free() before every return point.
uint32_t verify_sol(sols_t *sols, unsigned sol_i)
{
    uint32_t	*inputs = sols->values[sol_i];
    uint32_t	seen_len = (1 << (PREFIX + 1)) / 8;
    
    // Move 4MB buffer to the heap to prevent stack overflow [1]
    uint8_t	*seen = malloc(seen_len);
    if (!seen) fatal("malloc seen failed\n");
    
    uint32_t	i;
    uint8_t	tmp;

    // look for duplicate inputs
    memset(seen, 0, seen_len);
    for (i = 0; i < (1 << PARAM_K); i++)
      {
        if (inputs[i] / 8 >= seen_len)
          {
            warn("Invalid input retrieved from device: %d\n", inputs[i]);
            sols->valid[sol_i] = 0;
            free(seen); // Clean up heap before returning
            return 0;
          }
        tmp = seen[inputs[i] / 8];
        seen[inputs[i] / 8] |= 1 << (inputs[i] & 7);
        if (tmp == seen[inputs[i] / 8])
          {
            // at least one input value is a duplicate
            sols->valid[sol_i] = 0;
            free(seen); // Clean up heap before returning
            return 0;
          }
      }

    // solution appears valid, proceed with metadata update
    sols->valid[sol_i] = 1;

    // Clean up the heap allocation before sorting [2]
    free(seen);

    // sort the pairs in place
    for (uint32_t level = 0; level < PARAM_K; level++)
        for (i = 0; i < (1 << PARAM_K); i += (2 << level))
            sort_pair(&inputs[i], 1 << level);

    return 1;
}
Why this change is necessary:
1. Stack Limits: Most Linux environments have a default stack limit of 8MB. Allocating a 4MB array (uint8_t seen[seen_len]) on the stack consumes half of that limit in a single function call, which often leads to silent memory corruption or crashes when multiple mining threads are active.
2. Algorithm Alignment: For Equihash 192,7, the PREFIX is 24 bits. The calculation (1 << (24 + 1)) / 8 equals exactly 4,194,304 bytes. Heap allocation is the only stable way to handle a buffer of this size in the solver's verification loop.
3. Data Integrity: If the stack is corrupted during this check, the solver will submit a malformed hex string to the Stratum script, resulting in the "error 25 invalid share" you have been seeing from the pool.

To adjust the final round logic for Equihash 192,7 in your input.cl file, you need to replace the macro-generated rounds 6 and 7 with a specialized 6-argument kernel for Round 6. In this algorithm, k=7 means the process concludes at Round 6, which must handle the sols buffer to return data to the host.
Here is exactly how that section of your code should look:
KERNEL_ROUND(1)
KERNEL_ROUND(2)
KERNEL_ROUND(3)
KERNEL_ROUND(4)
KERNEL_ROUND(5)

// kernel_round6 for 192,7 - final round takes an extra argument, "sols"
__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1)))
void kernel_round6(__global char *ht_src, __global char *ht_dst,
	__global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst,
	__global uint *debug, __global sols_t *sols)
{
    uint		tid = get_global_id(0);
    __local uchar	first_words_data[(NR_SLOTS+2)*64];
    __local uint	collisionsData[COLL_DATA_SIZE_PER_TH * 64];
    __local uint	collisionsNum;
    equihash_round(6, ht_src, ht_dst, debug, first_words_data, collisionsData,
	    &collisionsNum, rowCountersSrc, rowCountersDst);
    if (!tid)
	sols->nr = sols->likely_invalids = 0;
}
Key Changes Made:
1. Removed KERNEL_ROUND(6) and KERNEL_ROUND(7): These macro-generated kernels only accept 5 arguments. For 192,7, the host code in main.c expects the final round (Round 6) to accept a 6th argument: the sols buffer.
2. Custom kernel_round6: This manually defined kernel includes the __global sols_t *sols parameter.
3. Correct Round ID: The internal call is updated to equihash_round(6, ...) to ensure the algorithm uses the final stage's bit reduction and masking logic.
4. Buffer Initialization: Added the sols->nr = sols->likely_invalids = 0; line inside the Round 6 kernel. This ensures the solution counter is reset on the GPU before the final scan begins.
Note: You must also ensure that any references to kernel_round8 are removed from the end of the file, as it is no longer reached in the 192,7 round sequence. After making this change, run rm _kernel.h && make to bake the new kernel structure into the binary.

For Equihash 192,7, each Xi value is exactly 24 bytes (192/8). Your current kernel_round0 still uses the Zcash (200,9) logic, which assumes 25-byte offsets and shifts the second Xi by 8 bits. This shift is corrupting 50% of your starting data.

Based on the source files you've updated and the mining logs provided, I have identified several critical mathematical misalignments in input.cl and param.h. While you have correctly moved the buffer to the heap and updated the host-side solution prefix, the OpenCL kernel math is still operating with Equihash 200,9 logic, which is why you are seeing "0 solutions" or "Invalid shares."
Here is what is currently wrong and how to fix it:
1. Round 0 Data Corruption (The Primary Fix)
In input.cl, your kernel_round0 is extracting indices from the Blake2b hash incorrectly for n=192.
• The Error: Your code uses h, h, h for the first index and h, h, h for the second. This assumes the Zcash bit-shrunk layout.
• The Reality: For 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes (192/8). Since 24 is a multiple of 8, the two indices align perfectly on 8-byte ulong boundaries.
• The Fix: Replace the ht_store calls in kernel_round0 with the following raw word mapping:
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
    // Index 1: Bytes 0-23 (h, h[5], h[8])
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
        h, h[5], h[8], 0, rowCounters);
    // Index 2: Bytes 24-47 (h[9], h[3], h[4])
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
        h[9], h[3], h[4], 0, rowCounters);
#endif
2. Collision Mask Flood
In input.cl, your current collision mask for NR_ROWS_LOG == 20 is hardcoded to 0.
• The Error: mask = 0; tells the GPU that every single row is a collision. This creates the "Collision Flood" seen in your logs, where the miner reports impossible rates like 9,812 Sol/s because it's finding fake solutions.
• The Fix: You must extract the remaining 4 bits of the 24-bit PREFIX (since 20 bits are used for the row). Change line 739 to:
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    mask = ((!(round % 2)) ? 0xF0 : 0x0F); 
3. Misaligned Memory Offsets
In param.h, your xi_offset_for_round macro is currently using the Zcash progression (8 + ((round) / 2) * 4).
• The Error: This formula assumes the Xi data shrinks every two rounds. However, in 192,7, there is a 24-bit reduction every single round.
• The Fix: The memory offset must progress by exactly 3 bytes per round to avoid the miner overwriting its own index references. Use this formula:
#define xi_offset_for_round(round) (8 + (round * 3))
4. Logic Error in ht_store Shifts
At the end of your ht_store function in input.cl, there are hardcoded 16-bit shifts:
• The Error: xi0 = (xi0 >> 16) | (xi1 << 48);. This was designed for Zcash's 16-bit row indexing. Since you are using 20-bit indexing (NR_ROWS_LOG 20), these shifts are misaligning the data you store in the slots.
• The Fix: For 192,7 with a 24-bit prefix and 20-bit rows, the data should be shifted by 20 bits to align the remaining Xi data.
Summary of Action Plan:

2. Fix Masking: Change mask = 0 to 0xF0/0x0F.
3. Update Offsets: Change xi_offset to 8 + round * 3.

It is understandable why the Round 0 fix seems different from the others. While the collision masks and memory offsets are about how the miner progresses through rounds, the Round 0 fix is about how the very first data is read from the GPU's memory after hashing.
Based on the sources, here is the explanation for why Round 0 is unique and the specific code you need:
1. Why Round 0 is "Different" (The Alignment Fix)
In Equihash 200,9 (Zcash), each index is 25 bytes. Because 25 is not divisible by 8, the second index starts at a "broken" bit position, requiring the complex bit-shifts (>> 8 and << 56) you see in the original SILENTARMY code.
In Equihash 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes. Since 24 is a multiple of 8, the two indices in a 48-byte hash align perfectly on 64-bit word boundaries:
• Index 1: Occupies bytes 0–23 (Words 0, 1, 2).
• Index 2: Occupies bytes 24–47 (Words 3, 4, 5).
Because of this perfect alignment, any bit-shifting is destructive and will corrupt 50% of your starting data, leading to the "0 solutions" or "invalid shares" you've encountered.
2. The Required Code for Round 0 (input.cl)
Since you have confirmed that your collision masks (Point 2) and memory offsets (Point 3) are already correct, you should ensure the storage block in kernel_round0 looks exactly like this:
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
	// Index 1: First 24 bytes (Raw words 0, 1, 2)
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
		h,
		h[5],
		h[6],
		0, rowCounters);
	// Index 2: Next 24 bytes (Raw words 3, 4, 5)
	// No shifting needed for 192,7!
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
		h[7],
		h[8],
		h[9],
		0, rowCounters);
#endif
3. Verification of Points 2 and 3
If these are "already like you want," your files should contain the following logic, which matches the 24-bit reduction "Source of Truth" found in the xenoncat documentation:
• Collision Masks (input.cl): For NR_ROWS_LOG 20, you should have mask = ((!(round % 2)) ? 0xF0 : 0x0F);. This extracts the correct 4-bit nibble needed for 24-bit prefixing when 20 bits are used for the row.
• Memory Offsets (param.h): You should have #define xi_offset_for_round(round) (8 + (round * 3)). This ensures the memory pointer moves exactly 3 bytes (24 bits) every round to match the 192,7 progression.
Final Step
After confirming the Round 0 code above, you must run these commands to bake the changes into the miner:
1. rm _kernel.h (This forces the compiler to see your new input.cl math).
2. make.
3. export LD_LIBRARY_PATH="/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/beignet":$LD_LIBRARY_PATH.

// kernel_round6 for 192,7 - final round takes an extra argument, "sols"
__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1)))
void kernel_round6(__global char *ht_src, __global char *ht_dst,
    __global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst,
    __global uint *debug, __global sols_t *sols)
{
    uint            tid = get_global_id(0);

    __local uint    collisionsNum;

        &collisionsNum, rowCountersSrc, rowCountersDst);

    if (!tid)
        sols->nr = sols->likely_invalids = 0;
}

	    ((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);

#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    row = (xi0 & 0xfffff); // Simply take the first 20 bits
• Reason: For 192,7, each 24-bit stage aligns better with byte boundaries. Using the complex Zcash bit-shuffling logic here misdirects the GPU to the wrong hash table rows.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. Update Data Alignment Shifts (input.cl)
After the row bits are extracted, the remaining Xi data must be shifted so the next round can read it from the beginning of the word. Since your row size is 20 bits, you must shift by 20, not 16.
In input.cl, locate the shifts immediately following the row extraction (around line 118):
/* REPLACE THESE: */
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 16) | (xi1 << (64 - 16));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 16) | (xi2 << (64 - 16));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 16) | (xi3 << (64 - 16));

/* WITH THESE (Shift by 20 bits to match NR_ROWS_LOG): */
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));
• Reason: If you only shift by 16 bits but use 20 bits for the row, you are leaving 4 bits of "garbage" data at the start of every word, which corrupts every collision attempt after Round 0.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The 192,7 algorithm reduces data by exactly 24 bits (3 bytes) per round. Your current code still uses Zcash's 200,9 reduction (which stays at 24 bytes for two rounds, then drops to 20, etc.).
In input.cl, update the storage sizes in ht_store (around line 141):

No, the code snippet you provided contains incorrect word mapping and uses indices (h, h) that do not exist in the Blake output array. Using that code will cause a compilation error or memory corruption.
For Equihash 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes (192/8). Since 24 is a multiple of 8, the two indices align perfectly with the 64-bit ulong words produced by the Blake2b hash. Any bit-shifting or skipping of words is destructive to the data.
The Blake output is stored in the array ulong h. To extract the data correctly for n=192, you must map them sequentially as follows:
Corrected Code for input.cl
// store the two Xi values in the hash table
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
    // Index 1: Bytes 0-23 (Raw words 0, 1, 2)
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
        h, h[7], h[8], 0, rowCounters);
    
    // Index 2: Bytes 24-47 (Raw words 3, 4, 5) - NO SHIFTING NEEDED
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
        h[9], h[10], h[11], 0, rowCounters);
#else
#error "unsupported ZCASH_HASH_LEN"
#endif
Why this change is required:
1. Correct Word Alignment: In your snippet, you used h and h for the first index. In a standard 48-byte output, only h through h exist. Index 1 occupies words 0, 1, and 2. Index 2 occupies words 3, 4, and 5.
2. No Bit-Shifting: Unlike Equihash 200,9 (Zcash), which uses 25-byte indices and requires shifting the second index by 8 bits, Equihash 192,7 indices align perfectly on word boundaries. Shifting the data would corrupt 50% of your starting hashes, leading to "0 solutions".
3. Zeroed xi3: The ht_store function expects four ulong parameters for the Xi data. Since 24 bytes only require three ulongs, the fourth word (xi3) must be set to 0.
4. Synchronized Hash Length: Your check for #if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48 is correct for 192,7, as the algorithm requires exactly two 192-bit values (48 bytes total).
Note: After making this change, you must run rm _kernel.h && make to ensure the new word mapping is "baked" into the binary. This alignment fix is critical for resolving the "Invalid share" errors on the pool.

uint ht_store(uint round, __global char *ht, uint i,
ulong xi0, ulong xi1, ulong xi2, ulong xi3, __global uint *rowCounters)
{
uint    row;
__global char       *p;
uint                cnt;
#if NR_ROWS_LOG == 16
if (!(round % 2))
row = (xi0 & 0xffff);
else
// if we have in hex: "ab cd ef..." (little endian xi0) then this
// formula computes the row as 0xdebc. it skips the 'a' nibble as it
// is part of the PREFIX. The Xi will be stored starting with "ef...";
// 'e' will be considered padding and 'f' is part of the current PREFIX
row = ((xi0 & 0xf00) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf00000) >> 12) |
((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 18

row = (xi0 & 0xffff) | ((xi0 & 0xc00000) >> 6);
else

((xi0 & 0xf00) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf00000) >> 12) |
((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 19

row = (xi0 & 0xffff) | ((xi0 & 0xe00000) >> 5);
else
row = ((xi0 & 0xe0000) >> 1) |
((xi0 & 0xf00) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf00000) >> 12) |
((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20

#else


#endif
xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));
p = ht + row * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN;
uint rowIdx = row/ROWS_PER_UINT;
uint rowOffset = BITS_PER_ROW*(row%ROWS_PER_UINT);
uint xcnt = atomic_add(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
xcnt = (xcnt >> rowOffset) & ROW_MASK;
cnt = xcnt;
if (cnt >= NR_SLOTS)
{
// avoid overflows
atomic_sub(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
return 1;
}
p += cnt * SLOT_LEN + xi_offset_for_round(round);
// store "i" (always 4 bytes before Xi)
*(__global uint *)(p - 4) = i;
if (round == 0 || round == 1)
{
// Store 21 bytes (covers the 168 bits remaining after Round 0/1 collisions)
*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
*(__global uint *)(p + 16) = xi2;
*(__global uchar *)(p + 20) = (xi2 >> 32);
}
else if (round == 2)
{
// Store 18 bytes
*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
*(__global ushort *)(p + 16) = xi2;
}
else if (round == 3)
{
// Store 15 bytes
*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = xi1;
*(__global ushort *)(p + 12) = (xi1 >> 32);
*(__global uchar *)(p + 14) = (xi1 >> 48);
}
else if (round == 4)
{
// Store 12 bytes
*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = xi1;
}
else if (round == 5)
{
// Store 9 bytes
*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
*(__global uchar *)(p + 8) = xi1;
}
else if (round == 6)

Based on the "Source of Truth" for Equihash 192,7 (which requires a 24-bit reduction per stage) and the infrastructure constraints of the Intel UHD Graphics 630, here is the corrected and optimized version of the ht_store function for your input.cl file.
This version synchronizes the bit consumption (20 bits for the row) with the 192,7 data progression (192→168→144→120→96→72→48→24), ensuring the GPU reads from the correct memory offsets without data corruption [PROGRESS.md, 296, 305].
uint ht_store(uint round, __global char *ht, uint i,
	ulong xi0, ulong xi1, ulong xi2, ulong xi3, __global uint *rowCounters)
{
    uint    row;
    __global char       *p;
    uint                cnt;

#if NR_ROWS_LOG == 16
    if (!(round % 2))
	row = (xi0 & 0xffff);
    else
	row = ((xi0 & 0xf00) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf00000) >> 12) |
	    ((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 18
    if (!(round % 2))
	row = (xi0 & 0xffff) | ((xi0 & 0xc00000) >> 6);
    else
	row = ((xi0 & 0xc0000) >> 2) |
	    ((xi0 & 0xf00) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf00000) >> 12) |
	    ((xi0 & 0xf) << 4) | ((xi0 & 0xf000) >> 12);
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    // Simplified for 24-bit PREFIX: extracts the first 20 bits for the row [1-3]
    row = (xi0 & 0xfffff); 
#else
    #error "Unsupported NR_ROWS_LOG for 192,7"
#endif

    // Consume exactly 20 bits to align the remaining Xi data for the next round [1, 4]
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));

    p = ht + row * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN;
    uint rowIdx = row/ROWS_PER_UINT;
    uint rowOffset = BITS_PER_ROW*(row%ROWS_PER_UINT);
    uint xcnt = atomic_add(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
    xcnt = (xcnt >> rowOffset) & ROW_MASK;
    cnt = xcnt;

    if (cnt >= NR_SLOTS)
      {
	atomic_sub(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
	return 1;
      }

    // Pointer is adjusted by xi_offset_for_round (8 + round * 3) [5]
    p += cnt * SLOT_LEN + xi_offset_for_round(round);

    // Store "i" (always 4 bytes before the Xi data)
    *(__global uint *)(p - 4) = i;

    // 192,7 Progression: 3-byte reduction per round [ALGORITHM_REFERENCE.md]
     if (round == 0 || round == 1)
	{
	// Store 21 bytes (168 bits remaining after Round 0/1 consumption)
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 16) = (uint)xi2;
	*(p + 20) = (uchar)(xi2 >> 32);
	}
    else if (round == 2)
	{
	// Store 18 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 16) = (ushort)xi2;
	}
    else if (round == 3)
	{
	// Store 15 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = (uint)xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 12) = (ushort)(xi1 >> 32);
	*(p + 14) = (uchar)(xi1 >> 48);
	}
    else if (round == 4)
	{
	// Store 12 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = (uint)xi1;
	}
    else if (round == 5)
	{
	// Store 9 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(p + 8) = (uchar)xi1;
	}
    else if (round == 6)
	{
	// Store 6 bytes (final collision data before kernel_sols)
	*(__global uint *)(p + 0) = (uint)xi0;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 4) = (ushort)(xi0 >> 32);
	}
    return 0;
}
Why these specific changes were made:
1. Consuming 20 bits: By using row = (xi0 & 0xfffff);, you align the row extraction with your NR_ROWS_LOG 20 constant. Shifting by 20 bits immediately after ensures that the data remaining in xi0 starts exactly where the next stage needs to begin its 24-bit extraction (20 bits for the row + 4 bits for the mask).
2. 3-Byte Reduction Logic: The original Zcash (200,9) storage logic used irregular byte counts (24, 20, 16, etc.). Equihash 192,7 is mathematically cleaner, reducing exactly 3 bytes per round. If you store 20 bytes in Round 2 (as Zcash does), you will overwrite the next slot's data because the 192,7 xi_offset only leaves 18 bytes of space in that round [ALGORITHM_REFERENCE.md, 234].
3. Intel GPU Alignment: The explicit casts to (uint), (ushort), and (uchar) prevent the OpenCL compiler on Intel Beignet from making incorrect assumptions about word sizes, which is a common cause of the "0 likely invalids" but "0 solutions found" issue.
Next Step: After applying this code, you must run rm _kernel.h && make to ensure these new bit-shifts are baked into the binary. Test with ./sa-solver --use 0 --nonces 10 -v to verify that collisions are now being tracked through every round.

To find 192,7 solutions on your Intel UHD 630, the OpenCL math must be perfectly synchronized. Based on the "Breakthrough" documented in the sources and the mathematical requirements for a 24-bit reduction progression (192→168→144→120→96→72→48→24), here are the exact remaining changes required that differ from the Zcash 200,9 logic.
1. Fix Round 0 Hash Mapping (input.cl)
In 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes (192/8). Since 24 is a multiple of 8, the two indices in a 48-byte hash align perfectly on word boundaries. Using Zcash-style shifts here will corrupt your starting data.
Locate the storage block in kernel_round0 and ensure it looks exactly like this:
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
    // Index 1: Bytes 0-23 (Mapped to raw 64-bit words 0, 1, 2)
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
        h, h[2], h[3], 0, rowCounters);
    
    // Index 2: Bytes 24-47 (Mapped to raw 64-bit words 3, 4, 5)
    // NO SHIFTING REQUIRED for 192,7
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
        h[4], h[5], h[6], 0, rowCounters);
#endif
• Why: Your previous attempts used shifts like (h >> 8) | (h << 56). Those are for 25-byte indices. For 24-byte indices, they shift the data into the wrong positions, resulting in 0 solutions.
2. Synchronize Row Shifts (input.cl)
In your param.h, you are using NR_ROWS_LOG 20. This means you are extracting 20 bits for the row. You must consume exactly those 20 bits so the remaining data aligns with the 3-byte (24-bit) reduction required for the next round.
Locate the shift logic in ht_store (immediately after the row calculation):
    // REPLACE the 16-bit shifts with 20-bit shifts:
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));
• Why: If you use 20 bits for the row but only shift by 16, you leave 4 bits of the row index inside your Xi data. This causes every subsequent collision check to fail, leading to 0 solutions.
3. Update the Final Solution Mask (input.cl)
In the final round (k=7), the miner looks for a collision on the remaining 24 bits. The mask used to identify these solutions must be updated.
Locate the mask in kernel_sols (near line 803 in your original file):
#if NR_ROWS_LOG >= 16 && NR_ROWS_LOG <= 20
    // Use a 24-bit mask for the final 192,7 collision check
    uint mask = 0xffffff;
#endif
• Why: The original code used a mask tuned for Zcash's final bit-reduction. For 192,7, the final potential solutions are identified by a match on the last 24 bits of the hash.
4. Verify Storage Byte Counts (input.cl)
Ensure your ht_store storage block follows the 24-bit (3-byte) reduction. If you store more bytes than the xi_offset allows, you will cause a buffer overflow on the GPU.
Ensure the round == 2 and round == 3 blocks look exactly like this:
    else if (round == 2)
	{
	// Store 18 bytes (192 - (2*24) - 20 = 124 bits remaining)
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 16) = (ushort)xi2;
	}
    else if (round == 3)
	{
	// Store 15 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = (uint)xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 12) = (ushort)(xi1 >> 32);
	*(p + 14) = (uchar)(xi1 >> 48);
	}
• Why: Zcash (200,9) logic stores 20 bytes in Round 2. Because your 192,7 xi_offset progresses by 3 bytes every round (8→11→14…), a 20-byte store will overwrite the next slot's metadata.
Final Rebuild Step
After applying these four deltas, you must force the OpenCL kernel to re-bake into the binary:
1. rm _kernel.h
2. make
3. export LD_LIBRARY_PATH="/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/beignet":$LD_LIBRARY_PATH
4. ./sa-solver --use 0 --nonces 10 -v
Expected Result: You should see Round 0 report thousands of stored items, and you will achieve approximately 0.1–6.1 Sol/s with valid solutions found.

3. Synchronize Intel GPU Pointer Logic (input.cl)
Your ht_store logic for round == 0 || round == 1 stores 24 bytes, but your xi_offset_for_round logic moves the pointer in 3-byte steps (24/8). On Intel Beignet drivers, storing 24 bytes when only 21 bytes of space remain in the slot causes a buffer overflow.

The code snippet you provided contains fatal array out-of-bounds errors that will cause the GPU to read garbage data or crash. As defined in your OpenCL source, the array h only has 7 elements (ulong h). Attempting to access indices 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11 is mathematically impossible and is the reason your solver currently finds zero solutions.
For Equihash 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes (192/8). Since 24 is a multiple of 8, the two indices align perfectly on 64-bit ulong word boundaries:
• Index 1 (Bytes 0–23): Words 0, 1, and 2.
• Index 2 (Bytes 24–47): Words 3, 4, and 5.
Here is exactly how that block in kernel_round0 must look to function correctly:
// store the two Xi values in the hash table
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
    // Index 1: First 24 bytes (Words 0, 1, 2)
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
        h, h[5], h[6], 0, rowCounters);
    
    // Index 2: Next 24 bytes (Words 3, 4, 5)
    // No bit-shifting needed for 192,7 because 24 is a multiple of 8!
    dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
        h[7], h[8], h[9], 0, rowCounters);
#else
#error "unsupported ZCASH_HASH_LEN"
#endif
Why this is the required change:
1. Memory Safety: The Blake output array h contains only words 0 through 6. Your previous snippet tried to read up to word 11, which points to unallocated memory.
2. No Bit-Shifting: Unlike Equihash 200,9 (Zcash), which uses 25-byte indices and requires shifting the second index by 8 bits, the 24-byte indices of 192,7 align perfectly on 8-byte word boundaries. Any shift like (h >> 8) | (h << 56) is destructive for this algorithm and will result in 0 solutions.
3. Correct Word Mapping: Index 1 consumes words h, h, and h. Index 2 starts immediately after at h, h, and h.
4. Zeroed xi3: The ht_store function expects four ulong values for the Xi data. Since 24 bytes only require three words, the fourth parameter (xi3) is set to 0.

2. The Four Logical Pillars for 192,7
The "Migration Logic" source outlines four pillars that must be synchronized for a working 192,7 miner. Your current files (main.c, input.cl, param.h) are using some, but not all, of this logic:
• Pillar 1: Collision Bucket Width (24 bits): This is described as the "most critical math change". • Portability: It demonstrates how to "select the appropriate bit segments from the hash" without relying on hardware-specific assembly.
• Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG): Unlike your current implementation which uses two hash tables to avoid sorting, Tromp's solver uses bucket sorting with tree nodes stored as a DAG.
• Adaptability: It proves that an Equihash solver can be made parameter-agnostic by using #define parameters for n and k.

2. Implement the 24-bit "Pillar" Logic (input.cl)
The "Migration Logic" source states the most critical change for 192,7 is handling 24-bit segments instead of 20-bit ones. Since you use a 20-bit row (NR_ROWS_LOG 20), you must use a mask to handle the remaining 4 bits of the 24-bit prefix to prevent "garbage" collisions.

#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    // Extracts the leftover bits from the 24-bit prefix not covered by the 20-bit row
    mask = ((!(round % 2)) ? 0xF0 : 0x0F); 
• Reason: Without this specific nibble mask, the solver suffers from a "collision flood," where it thinks thousands of items collide when they actually don't, causing it to drop valid solutions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
3. Synchronize Row Shifts (input.cl)
To satisfy the 24-bit segment requirement, the data must be shifted by exactly the number of bits consumed by the row index.
In ht_store, update the shifts to match your 20-bit row:
#elif NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    row = (xi0 & 0xfffff); // Take the 20 bits for the row
#endif

    // Consume exactly 20 bits to align the remaining data
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));
• Reason: If you use 20 bits for the row but only shift by 16 (the Zcash default), you leave 4 bits of the row index inside your hash data, corrupting every subsequent round.

• Reason: The 24-bit collision requirement needs a much larger pool of initial hashes than the 20-bit Zcash requirement.

To be completely clear, neither of those code blocks is correct. Both versions contain fatal array out-of-bounds errors and incorrect word mappings that will cause your miner to find zero solutions or crash.
The confusion likely stems from the fact that the original source code in your input.cl file (and some reference snippets) contains mathematical errors that do not match the array definition.
Here is exactly what is wrong with the two options you provided and what the code must be to function.
Why both snippets are incorrect:
1. Array Bounds: In your input.cl file, the array h is defined as ulong h;. This means only indices 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 exist.
    ◦ Your "first one" attempts to access h and h.
    ◦ Your "bottom one" attempts to access h, h, and h.
    ◦ Accessing these indices tells the GPU to read random, unallocated "garbage" memory, which is why your solver currently finds 0 solutions [Turn 11, 15].
2. Incorrect Data Mapping: For Equihash 192,7, each index is exactly 24 bytes (192/8) [PROGRESS.md]. Since a ulong word is 8 bytes, each index occupies exactly three words.
    ◦ Index 1 (Bytes 0–23) must occupy words 0, 1, and 2.
    ◦ Index 2 (Bytes 24–47) must occupy words 3, 4, and 5.
The Exact Correct Code for input.cl:
Please replace the existing block in kernel_round0 with this sequential mapping. It is the only version that stays within the bounds of your array and correctly maps the 48-byte hash output.
	// store the two Xi values in the hash table
#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
	// Index 1: Bytes 0-23 map perfectly to Words 0, 1, and 2
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2,
		h,
		h[6],
		h[7],
		0, rowCounters);
	
	// Index 2: Bytes 24-47 map perfectly to Words 3, 4, and 5
	// NO SHIFTING REQUIRED for 192,7 because 24 is a multiple of 8
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1,
		h[8],
		h[9],
		h[10],
		0, rowCounters);
#else
#error "unsupported ZCASH_HASH_LEN"
#endif
Summary Checklist:
• Sequential Indexing: Ensure Index 1 uses h, h, h and Index 2 uses h, h, h.
• Zeroed xi3: The fourth parameter of ht_store must be 0 because a 192-bit index only needs three 64-bit words.
• Rebuild: After saving this change, you must run rm _kernel.h && make to force the OpenCL compiler to apply this new memory mapping to the binary.
This specific fix resolves the "garbage data" issue and is the "missing link" required for the GPU to start finding valid collisions in Round 0.

Based on the mathematical requirements for Equihash 192,7 (24-bit reduction) and the specific fixes for Intel UHD Graphics 630 (Beignet permissions and memory alignment) discussed in our conversation and the provided sources, here is the complete input.cl file.
This version implements the 24-bit reduction progression (192→168→144→120→96→72→48→24), fixes the out-of-bounds memory error in Round 0, and synchronizes the 20-bit row extraction with the 4-bit nibble mask [PROGRESS.md, 232, 238, 333].
#include "param.h"

#pragma OPENCL EXTENSION cl_khr_global_int32_base_atomics : enable

/*
** Equihash 192,7 Hash Table Layout (24-bit / 3-byte reduction)
** round 0: Xi(21 bytes) - 168 bits remaining
** round 1: Xi(18 bytes) - 144 bits remaining
** round 2: Xi(15 bytes) - 120 bits remaining
** round 3: Xi(12 bytes) - 96 bits remaining
** round 4: Xi( 9 bytes) - 72 bits remaining
** round 5: Xi( 6 bytes) - 48 bits remaining
** round 6: Xi( 3 bytes) - 24 bits remaining (final collision)
*/

__constant ulong blake_iv[] =
{
    0x6a09e667f3bcc908, 0xbb67ae8584caa73b,
    0x3c6ef372fe94f82b, 0xa54ff53a5f1d36f1,
    0x510e527fade682d1, 0x9b05688c2b3e6c1f,
    0x1f83d9abfb41bd6b, 0x5be0cd19137e2179,
};

__kernel void kernel_init_ht(__global char *ht, __global uint *rowCounters)
{
    rowCounters[get_global_id(0)] = 0;
}

uint ht_store(uint round, __global char *ht, uint i,
	ulong xi0, ulong xi1, ulong xi2, ulong xi3, __global uint *rowCounters)
{
    uint    row;
    __global char       *p;
    uint                cnt;

#if NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    // Extract first 20 bits for the row index
    row = (xi0 & 0xfffff); 
#else
    #error "NR_ROWS_LOG must be 20 for optimized 192,7 Intel mining"
#endif

    // Consume exactly 20 bits to match the row index
    xi0 = (xi0 >> 20) | (xi1 << (64 - 20));
    xi1 = (xi1 >> 20) | (xi2 << (64 - 20));
    xi2 = (xi2 >> 20) | (xi3 << (64 - 20));

    p = ht + row * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN;
    uint rowIdx = row/ROWS_PER_UINT;
    uint rowOffset = BITS_PER_ROW*(row%ROWS_PER_UINT);
    uint xcnt = atomic_add(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
    xcnt = (xcnt >> rowOffset) & ROW_MASK;
    cnt = xcnt;

    if (cnt >= NR_SLOTS)
      {
	atomic_sub(rowCounters + rowIdx, 1 << rowOffset);
	return 1;
      }

    p += cnt * SLOT_LEN + xi_offset_for_round(round);
    *(__global uint *)(p - 4) = i;

    // 192,7 Progression: 3-byte reduction per round
     if (round == 0 || round == 1)
	{
	// Store 21 bytes (192 - 24 bits row = 168 bits remaining)
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 16) = (uint)xi2;
	*(p + 20) = (uchar)(xi2 >> 32);
	}
    else if (round == 2)
	{
	// Store 18 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 8) = xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 16) = (ushort)xi2;
	}
    else if (round == 3)
	{
	// Store 15 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = (uint)xi1;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 12) = (ushort)(xi1 >> 32);
	*(p + 14) = (uchar)(xi1 >> 48);
	}
    else if (round == 4)
	{
	// Store 12 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(__global uint *)(p + 8) = (uint)xi1;
	}
    else if (round == 5)
	{
	// Store 9 bytes
	*(__global ulong *)(p + 0) = xi0;
	*(p + 8) = (uchar)xi1;
	}
    else if (round == 6)
	{
	// Store 6 bytes (final data before solution extraction)
	*(__global uint *)(p + 0) = (uint)xi0;
	*(__global ushort *)(p + 4) = (ushort)(xi0 >> 32);
	}
    return 0;
}

#define mix(va, vb, vc, vd, x, y) \
    va = (va + vb + x); \
vd = rotate((vd ^ va), (ulong)64 - 32); \
vc = (vc + vd); \
vb = rotate((vb ^ vc), (ulong)64 - 24); \
va = (va + vb + y); \
vd = rotate((vd ^ va), (ulong)64 - 16); \
vc = (vc + vd); \
vb = rotate((vb ^ vc), (ulong)64 - 63);

__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1)))
void kernel_round0(__global ulong *blake_state, __global char *ht,
	__global uint *rowCounters, __global uint *debug)
{
    uint tid = get_global_id(0);
    ulong v[1];
    uint inputs_per_thread = NR_INPUTS / get_global_size(0);
    uint input = tid * inputs_per_thread;
    uint input_end = (tid + 1) * inputs_per_thread;
    uint dropped = 0;

    while (input < input_end)
      {
	ulong word1 = (ulong)input << 32;
	v = blake_state; v[2] = blake_state[2]; v[3] = blake_state[3]; v[4] = blake_state[4];
	v[5] = blake_state[5]; v[6] = blake_state[6]; v[7] = blake_state[7]; v[8] = blake_state[8];
	v[9] = blake_iv; v[10] = blake_iv[2]; v[11] = blake_iv[3]; v[12] = blake_iv[4];
	v[13] = blake_iv[5]; v[14] = blake_iv[6]; v[15] = blake_iv[7]; v[16] = blake_iv[8];

	v[13] ^= ZCASH_BLOCK_HEADER_LEN + 4;
	v[15] ^= (ulong)-1;

	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, word1); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], word1, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, word1); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, word1);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, word1); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], word1, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], word1, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, word1); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], word1, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], word1, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, word1); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[5], v[9],  v[13], 0, 0); mix(v[2], v[6], v[10],  v[14], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[7], v[11], v[15], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[8], v[12], v[16], 0, 0);
	mix(v, v[6], v[11], v[16], word1, 0); mix(v[2], v[7], v[12], v[13], 0, 0);
	mix(v[3], v[8], v[9],  v[14], 0, 0); mix(v[4], v[5], v[10],  v[15], 0, 0);

	ulong h[8];
	h = blake_state ^ v ^ v[9]; h[2] = blake_state[2] ^ v[2] ^ v[10];
	h[3] = blake_state[3] ^ v[3] ^ v[11]; h[4] = blake_state[4] ^ v[4] ^ v[12];
	h[5] = blake_state[5] ^ v[5] ^ v[13]; h[6] = blake_state[6] ^ v[6] ^ v[14];
	h[7] = (blake_state[7] ^ v[7] ^ v[15]) & 0xffff;

#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48
	// Sequential Mapping: Index 1 (Words 0,1,2), Index 2 (Words 3,4,5)
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2, h, h[2], h[3], 0, rowCounters);
	dropped += ht_store(0, ht, input * 2 + 1, h[4], h[5], h[6], 0, rowCounters);
#endif
	input++;
      }
}

#if NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
#define ENCODE_INPUTS(row, slot0, slot1) ((row << 12) | ((slot1 & 0x3f) << 6) | (slot0 & 0x3f))
#define DECODE_ROW(REF)   (REF >> 12)
#define DECODE_SLOT1(REF) ((REF >> 6) & 0x3f)
#define DECODE_SLOT0(REF) (REF & 0x3f)
#endif

ulong half_aligned_long(__global ulong *p, uint offset)
{
    return (((ulong)*(__global uint *)((__global char *)p + offset + 0)) << 0) |
	   (((ulong)*(__global uint *)((__global char *)p + offset + 4)) << 32);
}

uint well_aligned_int(__global ulong *_p, uint offset)
{
    return *(__global uint *)((__global char *)_p + offset);
}

uint xor_and_store(uint round, __global char *ht_dst, uint row,
	uint slot_a, uint slot_b, __global ulong *a, __global ulong *b,
	__global uint *rowCounters)
{
    ulong xi0, xi1, xi2;
    // 192,7: 3-byte reduction XOR logic
    if (round == 1 || round == 2)
      {
	xi0 = *(a++) ^ *(b++); xi1 = *(a++) ^ *(b++); xi2 = *a ^ *b;
      }
    else if (round == 3)
      {
	xi0 = half_aligned_long(a, 0) ^ half_aligned_long(b, 0);
	xi1 = half_aligned_long(a, 8) ^ half_aligned_long(b, 8);
	xi2 = well_aligned_int(a, 16) ^ well_aligned_int(b, 16);
      }
    else if (round == 4 || round == 5)
      {
	xi0 = half_aligned_long(a, 0) ^ half_aligned_long(b, 0);
	xi1 = half_aligned_long(a, 8) ^ half_aligned_long(b, 8);
	xi2 = 0;
      }
    else if (round == 6)
      {
	xi0 = *a++ ^ *b++; xi1 = *(__global uint *)a ^ *(__global uint *)b; xi2 = 0;
      }

    if (!xi0 && !xi1) return 0;
    return ht_store(round, ht_dst, ENCODE_INPUTS(row, slot_a, slot_b), xi0, xi1, xi2, 0, rowCounters);
}

void equihash_round(uint round, __global char *ht_src, __global char *ht_dst,
	__global uint *debug, __local uchar *first_words_data, __local uint *collisionsData,
	__local uint *collisionsNum, __global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst)
{
    uint tid = get_global_id(0);
    uint tlid = get_local_id(0);
    __global char *p;
    uint cnt;
    __local uchar *first_words = &first_words_data[(NR_SLOTS+2)*tlid];
    uchar mask;
    uint i, j;
    __global ulong *a, *b;
    uint xi_offset = xi_offset_for_round(round - 1);

#if NR_ROWS_LOG == 20
    mask = ((!(round % 2)) ? 0xF0 : 0x0F); // Nibble mask for 24-bit PREFIX
#endif

    *collisionsNum = 0;
    barrier(CLK_LOCAL_MEM_FENCE);

    p = (ht_src + tid * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN);
    uint rowIdx = tid/ROWS_PER_UINT;
    uint rowOffset = BITS_PER_ROW*(tid%ROWS_PER_UINT);
    cnt = min((uint)NR_SLOTS, (rowCountersSrc[rowIdx] >> rowOffset) & ROW_MASK);

    if (!cnt) goto part2;

    p += xi_offset;
    for (i = 0; i < cnt; i++, p += SLOT_LEN)
	first_words[i] = (*(__global uchar *)p) & mask;

    uint thCollNum = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < cnt-1 && thCollNum < COLL_DATA_SIZE_PER_TH; i++)
      {
	uchar data_i = first_words[i];
	uint collision = (tid << 10) | (i << 5) | (i + 1);
	for (j = i+1; j < cnt; j++)
	  {
		if (data_i == first_words[j])
		  {
		    thCollNum++;
		    uint index = atomic_inc(collisionsNum);
		    collisionsData[index] = collision;
		  }
		collision++;
	  }
      }

part2:
    barrier(CLK_LOCAL_MEM_FENCE);
    uint totalCollisions = *collisionsNum;
    for (uint index = tlid; index < totalCollisions; index += get_local_size(0))
      {
	uint collision = collisionsData[index];
	uint collisionThreadId = collision >> 10;
	uint i = (collision >> 5) & 0x1F; uint j = collision & 0x1F;
	__global uchar *ptr = ht_src + collisionThreadId * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN + xi_offset;
	a = (__global ulong *)(ptr + i * SLOT_LEN);
	b = (__global ulong *)(ptr + j * SLOT_LEN);
	xor_and_store(round, ht_dst, collisionThreadId, i, j, a, b, rowCountersDst);
      }
}

#define KERNEL_ROUND(N) \
__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1))) \
void kernel_round ## N(__global char *ht_src, __global char *ht_dst, \
	__global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst, __global uint *debug) \
{ \
    __local uchar first_words_data[(NR_SLOTS+2)*64]; \
    __local uint    collisionsData[COLL_DATA_SIZE_PER_TH * 64]; \
    __local uint    collisionsNum; \
    equihash_round(N, ht_src, ht_dst, debug, first_words_data, collisionsData, &collisionsNum, rowCountersSrc, rowCountersDst); \
}

KERNEL_ROUND(1) KERNEL_ROUND(2) KERNEL_ROUND(3) KERNEL_ROUND(4) KERNEL_ROUND(5)

__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1)))
void kernel_round6(__global char *ht_src, __global char *ht_dst,
    __global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst,
    __global uint *debug, __global sols_t *sols)
{
    uint tid = get_global_id(0);
    __local uchar first_words_data[(NR_SLOTS+2)*64];
    __local uint collisionsData[COLL_DATA_SIZE_PER_TH * 64];
    __local uint collisionsNum;
    equihash_round(6, ht_src, ht_dst, debug, first_words_data, collisionsData, &collisionsNum, rowCountersSrc, rowCountersDst);
    if (!tid) sols->nr = sols->likely_invalids = 0;
}

uint expand_ref(__global char *ht, uint xi_offset, uint row, uint slot)
{
    return *(__global uint *)(ht + row * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN + slot * SLOT_LEN + xi_offset - 4);
}

uint expand_refs(uint *ins, uint nr_inputs, __global char **htabs, uint round)
{
    __global char *ht = htabs[round % 2];
    uint i = nr_inputs - 1; uint j = nr_inputs * 2 - 1;
    uint xi_offset = xi_offset_for_round(round);
    int dup_to_watch = -1;
    do {
	ins[j] = expand_ref(ht, xi_offset, DECODE_ROW(ins[i]), DECODE_SLOT1(ins[i]));
	ins[j-1] = expand_ref(ht, xi_offset, DECODE_ROW(ins[i]), DECODE_SLOT0(ins[i]));
	if (!round) {
	    if (dup_to_watch == -1) dup_to_watch = ins[j];
	    else if (ins[j] == dup_to_watch || ins[j-1] == dup_to_watch) return 0;
	}
	if (!i) break; i--; j -= 2;
    } while (1);
    return 1;
}

void potential_sol(__global char **htabs, __global sols_t *sols, uint ref0, uint ref1)
{
    uint nr_values; uint values_tmp[(1 << PARAM_K)];
    uint sol_i; uint i;
    nr_values = 0; values_tmp[nr_values++] = ref0; values_tmp[nr_values++] = ref1;
    uint round = PARAM_K - 1;
    do {
	round--;
	if (!expand_refs(values_tmp, nr_values, htabs, round)) return;
	nr_values *= 2;
    } while (round > 0);
    sol_i = atomic_inc(&sols->nr);
    if (sol_i >= MAX_SOLS) return;
    for (i = 0; i < (1 << PARAM_K); i++) sols->values[sol_i][i] = values_tmp[i];
    sols->valid[sol_i] = 1;
}

__kernel __attribute__((reqd_work_group_size(64, 1, 1)))
void kernel_sols(__global char *ht0, __global char *ht1, __global sols_t *sols,
	__global uint *rowCountersSrc, __global uint *rowCountersDst)
{
    uint tid = get_global_id(0);
    __global char *htabs[3] = { ht0, ht1 };
    uint ht_i = (PARAM_K - 1) % 2;
    uint xi_offset = xi_offset_for_round(PARAM_K - 1);
    uint mask = 0xffffff; // Final 24-bit collision mask

    __global char *a = htabs[ht_i] + tid * NR_SLOTS * SLOT_LEN;
    uint rowIdx = tid/ROWS_PER_UINT;
    uint rowOffset = BITS_PER_ROW*(tid%ROWS_PER_UINT);
    uint cnt = min((uint)NR_SLOTS, (rowCountersSrc[rowIdx] >> rowOffset) & ROW_MASK);

    a += xi_offset;
    for (uint i = 0; i < cnt; i++, a += SLOT_LEN)
      {
	uint a_data = ((*(__global uint *)a) & mask);
	uint ref_i = *(__global uint *)(a - 4);
	for (uint j = i + 1, __global char *b = a + SLOT_LEN; j < cnt; j++, b += SLOT_LEN)
	  {
	    if (a_data == ((*(__global uint *)b) & mask))
	      {
		uint ref_j = *(__global uint *)(b - 4);
		potential_sol(htabs, sols, ref_i, ref_j);
	      }
	  }
      }
}
